<ROLE>
Your primary role is to assist users by executing commands, modifying code, and solving technical problems effectively. You should be thorough, methodical, and prioritize quality over speed.
* If the user asks a question, like "why is X happening", don't try to fix the problem. Just give an answer to the question.
</ROLE>

<EFFICIENCY>
* Each action you take is somewhat expensive. Wherever possible, combine multiple actions into a single action, e.g. combine multiple bash commands into one, using sed and grep to edit/view multiple files at once.
* When exploring the codebase, use efficient tools like find, grep, and git commands with appropriate filters to minimize unnecessary operations.
</EFFICIENCY>

<FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>
* When a user provides a file path, do NOT assume it's relative to the current working directory. First explore the file system to locate the file before working on it.
* If asked to edit a file, edit the file directly, rather than creating a new file with a different filename.
* For global search-and-replace operations, consider using `sed` instead of opening file editors multiple times.
* NEVER create multiple versions of the same file with different suffixes (e.g., file_test.py, file_fix.py, file_simple.py). Instead:
  - Always modify the original file directly when making changes
  - If you need to create a temporary file for testing, delete it once you've confirmed your solution works
  - If you decide a file you created is no longer useful, delete it instead of creating a new version
* Do NOT include documentation files explaining your changes in version control unless the user explicitly requests it
* When reproducing bugs or implementing fixes, use a single file rather than creating multiple files with different versions
</FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>

<CODE_QUALITY>
* Write clean, efficient code with minimal comments. Avoid redundancy in comments: Do not repeat information that can be easily inferred from the code itself.
* When implementing solutions, focus on making the minimal changes needed to solve the problem.
* Before implementing any changes, first thoroughly understand the codebase through exploration.
* If you are adding a lot of code to a function or file, consider splitting the function or file into smaller pieces when appropriate.
* Place all imports at the top of the file unless explicitly requested otherwise or if placing imports at the top would cause issues (e.g., circular imports, conditional imports, or imports that need to be delayed for specific reasons).
</CODE_QUALITY>

<VERSION_CONTROL>
* If there are existing git user credentials already configured, use them and add Co-authored-by: openhands <openhands@all-hands.dev> to any commits messages you make. if a git config doesn't exist use "openhands" as the user.name and "openhands@all-hands.dev" as the user.email by default, unless explicitly instructed otherwise.
* Exercise caution with git operations. Do NOT make potentially dangerous changes (e.g., pushing to main, deleting repositories) unless explicitly asked to do so.
* When committing changes, use `git status` to see all modified files, and stage all files necessary for the commit. Use `git commit -a` whenever possible.
* Do NOT commit files that typically shouldn't go into version control (e.g., node_modules/, .env files, build directories, cache files, large binaries) unless explicitly instructed by the user.
* If unsure about committing certain files, check for the presence of .gitignore files or ask the user for clarification.
</VERSION_CONTROL>

<PULL_REQUESTS>

</PULL_REQUESTS>

1. EXPLORATION: Thoroughly explore relevant files and understand the context before proposing solutions

3. TESTING:

<ENVIRONMENT_SETUP>

  3. Only install individual packages directly if no dependency files are found or if only specific packages are needed

</ENVIRONMENT_SETUP>

  3. Methodically address the most likely causes, starting with the highest probability

</DOCUMENTATION>

<PROCESS_MANAGEMENT>
* When terminating processes:
  - Do NOT use general keywords with commands like `pkill -f server` or `pkill -f python` as this might accidentally kill other important servers or processes
  - Always use specific keywords that uniquely identify the target process
  - Prefer using `ps aux` to find the exact process ID (PID) first, then kill that specific PID
  - When possible, use more targeted approaches like finding the PID from a pidfile or using application-specific shutdown commands
</PROCESS_MANAGEMENT>

## Phase 1. READING: Understanding the Problem<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Let me analyze the issue description carefully:

1. Grammar definition:
   ```
   identifier = /\w+/ ;
   expr = mul | identifier ;
   mul = expr '*' identifier ;
   ```

2. Expected behavior:
   - Parsing `a * b` with start rule `expr` works correctly → `['a', '*', 'b']`
   - Parsing `a * b` with start rule `mul` should also work but fails

3. Error details:
   - The error occurs in `_mul_` method trying to match the token '*'
   - Stack trace shows recursive calling between `_expr_` and `_mul_` methods
   - Exception: `tatsu.exceptions.FailedToken: expecting '*'`

4. Root cause analysis:
   - This looks like a classic left recursion problem in parsers
   - When starting with `mul`, the parser tries to match `expr` first, which can match `mul`, leading to infinite recursion
   - The parser gets confused about where to place the '*' token

5. Technical details from stack trace:
   - Error originates in `/tatsu/contexts.py` line 203 in `parse` method
   - Recursive call handling in `_recursive_call` method (line 546)
   - Token matching failure in `_token` method (line 617)
   - Error raised in `_error` method (line 436)

The problem seems to be in how the parser handles left recursion detection and resolution, particularly when the left-recursive rule is the start rule.</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>### 1.1-1.3 Problem Analysis

The issue describes a **left recursion problem** in the TatSu parser. 

**Grammar Analysis:**
- `identifier = /\w+/` - matches word characters
- `expr = mul | identifier` - expression can be either multiplication or identifier  
- `mul = expr '*' identifier` - multiplication is expression followed by '*' and identifier

This creates a **left-recursive grammar** because `mul` starts with `expr`, and `expr` can start with `mul`.

**Problem Details:**
- ✅ Parsing `"a * b"` with start rule `expr` works: returns `['a', '*', 'b']`
- ❌ Parsing `"a * b"` with start rule `mul` fails with `FailedToken` exception expecting `'*'`

### 1.4 Steps to Reproduce
1. Define the grammar with left recursion as shown
2. Parse input `"a * b"` starting with rule `expr` → Success
3. Parse input `"a * b"` starting with rule `mul` → Failure

### 1.5 Best Practices for Testing/Fixing
- Understand TatSu's left recursion handling mechanism
- Test both entry points to ensure consistent behavior
- Preserve existing functionality for non-left-recursive cases
- Make minimal changes to core parsing logic

    16	    language and of Unix_
    17	
    18	|TatSu|
    19	=======
    20	
    21	|TatSu| (the successor to Grako_) is a tool that takes grammars in a
    22	variation of `EBNF`_ as input, and outputs `memoizing`_ (`Packrat`_)
    23	`PEG`_ parsers in `Python`_.
    24	
    25	|TatSu| can compile a grammar stored in a string into a
    26	``tatsu.grammars.Grammar`` object that can be used to parse any given
    27	input, much like the `re`_ module does with regular expressions, or it can generate a Python_ module that implements the parser.
    28	
    29	|TatSu| fully supports `left-recursive`_  rules in PEG_ grammars using the algorithm_ by *Laurent* and *Mens*. The generated AST_ has the expected left associativity.
    30	
    31	.. _algorithm: http://norswap.com/pubs/sle2016.pdf
    32	
    33	Installation
    34	------------
    35	
    36	.. code:: bash
    37	
    38	    $ pip install TatSu
    39	
    40	
    41	Using the Tool
    42	--------------
    43	
    44	|TatSu| can be used as a library, much like `Python`_'s ``re``, by embedding grammars as strings and generating grammar models instead of generating Python_ code.
    45	
    46	-   ``tatsu.compile(grammar, name=None, **kwargs)``
    47	
    48	    Compiles the grammar and generates a *model* that can subsequently be used for parsing input with.
    49	
    50	-   ``tatsu.parse(grammar, input, **kwargs)``
    51	
    52	    Compiles the grammar and parses the given input producing an AST_ as result. The result is equivalent to calling::
    53	
    54	        model = compile(grammar)
    55	        ast = model.parse(input)
    56	
    57	    Compiled grammars are cached for efficiency.
    58	
    59	-   ``tatsu.to_python_sourcecode(grammar, name=None, filename=None, **kwargs)``
    60	
    61	    Compiles the grammar to the `Python`_ sourcecode that implements the parser.
    62	
    63	This is an example of how to use |TatSu| as a library:
    64	
    65	.. code:: python
    66	
    67	    GRAMMAR = '''
    68	        @@grammar::CALC
    69	
    70	
    71	        start = expression $ ;
    72	
    73	
    74	        expression
    75	            =
    76	            | expression '+' term
    77	            | expression '-' term
    78	            | term
    79	            ;
    80	
    81	
    82	        term
    83	            =
    84	            | term '*' factor
    85	            | term '/' factor
    86	            | factor
    87	            ;
    88	
    89	
    90	        factor
    91	            =
    92	            | '(' expression ')'
    93	            | number
    94	            ;
    95	
    96	
    97	        number = /\d+/ ;
    98	    '''
    99	
   100	
   101	    if __name__ == '__main__':
   102	        import pprint
   103	        import json
   104	        from tatsu import parse
   105	
   106	        ast = parse(GRAMMAR, '3 + 5 * ( 10 - 20 )')
   107	        print('PPRINT')
   108	        pprint.pprint(ast, indent=2, width=20)
   109	        print()
   110	
   111	        print('JSON')
   112	        print(json.dumps(ast.asjson(), indent=2))
   113	        print()
   114	..
   115	
   116	And this is the output:
   117	
   118	.. code:: console
   119	
   120	    PPRINT
   121	    [ '3',
   122	      '+',
   123	      [ '5',
   124	        '*',
   125	        [ '10',
   126	          '-',
   127	          '20']]]
   128	
   129	    JSON
   130	    [
   131	      "3",
   132	      "+",
   133	      [
   134	        "5",
   135	        "*",
   136	        [
   137	          "10",
   138	          "-",
   139	          "20"
   140	        ]
   141	      ]
   142	    ]
   143

   181	    def test_nested_left_recursion(self, trace=False):
   182	        grammar_a = '''
   183	            @@left_recursion :: True
   184	            s = e $ ;
   185	            e = [e '+'] t ;
   186	            t = [t '*'] a ;
   187	            a = ?/[0-9]/? ;
   188	        '''
   189	        grammar_b = '''
   190	            @@left_recursion :: True
   191	            s = e $ ;
   192	            e = [e '+'] a ;
   193	            a = n | p ;
   194	            n = ?/[0-9]/? ;
   195	            p = '(' @:e ')' ;
   196	        '''
   197	        model_a = compile(grammar_a, "test")
   198	        model_b = compile(grammar_b, "test")
   199	        ast = model_a.parse("1*2+3*4", trace=trace, colorize=True)
   200	        self.assertEqual([['1', '*', '2'], '+', ['3', '*', '4']], ast)
   201	        ast = model_b.parse("(1+2)+(3+4)", trace=trace, colorize=True)
   202	        self.assertEqual([['1', '+', '2'], '+', ['3', '+', '4']], ast)
   203	        ast = model_a.parse("1*2*3", trace=trace, colorize=True)
   204	        self.assertEqual([['1', '*', '2'], '*', '3'], ast)
   205	        ast = model_b.parse("(((1+2)))", trace=trace, colorize=True)
   206	        self.assertEqual(['1', '+', '2'], ast)

   500	
   501	    def _set_left_recursion_guard(self, key):
   502	        ex = self._make_exception(key.name, exclass=FailedLeftRecursion)
   503	        self._memoize(key, ex)
   504	
   505	    def _call(self, ruleinfo):
   506	        self._rule_stack += [ruleinfo.name]
   507	        pos = self._pos
   508	        try:
   509	            self._trace_entry()
   510	
   511	            self._last_node = None
   512	
   513	            result = self._recursive_call(ruleinfo)
   514	            node, newpos, newstate = result
   515	
   516	            self._goto(newpos)
   517	            self._state = newstate
   518	            self._add_cst_node(node)
   519	            self._last_node = node
   520	
   521	            self._trace_success()
   522	
   523	            return node
   524	        except FailedPattern:
   525	            self._error('Expecting <%s>' % ruleinfo.name)
   526	        except FailedParse as e:
   527	            self._goto(pos)
   528	            self._set_furthest_exception(e)
   529	            self._trace_failure(e)
   530	            raise
   531	        finally:
   532	            self._rule_stack.pop()
   533	
   534	    def _clear_recursion_errors(self):
   535	        def filter(key, value):
   536	            return isinstance(value, FailedLeftRecursion)
   537	
   538	        prune_dict(self._memos, filter)
   539	
   540	    def _recursive_call(self, ruleinfo):
   541	        pos = self._pos
   542	        key = self.memokey
   543	
   544	        if key in self._results:
   545	            return self._results[key]
   546	        result = self._invoke_rule(ruleinfo, pos, key)
   547	
   548	        if not self._is_recursive(ruleinfo.name):
   549	            return result
   550	
</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Let me see more of the recursive call handling:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/neogeny__TatSu__4.2/tatsu/contexts.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>view</arg_value><arg_key>view_range</arg_key><arg_value>[540, 600]</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Here's the result of running `cat -n` on /workspace/neogeny__TatSu__4.2/tatsu/contexts.py:
   540	    def _recursive_call(self, ruleinfo):
   541	        pos = self._pos
   542	        key = self.memokey
   543	
   544	        if key in self._results:
   545	            return self._results[key]
   546	        result = self._invoke_rule(ruleinfo, pos, key)
   547	
   548	        if not self._is_recursive(ruleinfo.name):
   549	            return result
   550	
   551	        while True:
   552	            self._forget(key)
   553	            self._save_result(key, result.node)
   554	            self._clear_recursion_errors()
   555	            self._goto(pos)
   556	            try:
   557	                new_result = self._invoke_rule(ruleinfo, pos, key)
   558	            except FailedParse:
   559	                break
   560	            if self._pos <= result.newpos:
   561	                break
   562	            result = new_result
   563	
   564	        del self._results[key]
   565	        self._forget(key)
   566	        return result
   567	
   568	    def _invoke_rule(self, ruleinfo, pos, key):
   569	        memo = self._memo_for(key)
   570	        if isinstance(memo, Exception):
   571	            raise memo
   572	        elif memo:
   573	            return memo
   574	        self._set_left_recursion_guard(key)
   575	
   576	        self._push_ast()
   577	        try:
   578	            try:
   579	                self._next_token(ruleinfo)
   580	                ruleinfo.impl(self)
   581	                node = self._get_node(pos, ruleinfo)
   582	                node = self._invoke_semantic_rule(ruleinfo, node)
   583	                result = self._mkresult(node)
   584	                self._memoize(key, result)
   585	                return result
   586	            except FailedSemantics as e:
   587	                self._error(ustr(e), FailedParse)
   588	            finally:
   589	                self._pop_ast()
   590	        except FailedParse as e:
   591	            self._memoize(key, e)
   592	            self._goto(pos)

Now let me create a script to reproduce the issue:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/neogeny__TatSu__4.2/reproduce_issue.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Reproduction script for the left recursion issue described in the GitHub issue.
"""

from tatsu import parse
from tatsu.tool import compile

def test_issue():
    # Define the grammar from the issue
    grammar = '''
        identifier = /\w+/ ;
        expr = mul | identifier ;
        mul = expr '*' identifier ;
    '''
    
    input_text = "a * b"
    
    print("Testing grammar:")
    print(grammar)
    print(f"Input: {input_text}")
    print()
    
    # Compile the grammar
    model = compile(grammar, "TestGrammar")
    
    # Test 1: Parse with start rule 'expr' (should work)
    print("=== Test 1: Parsing with start rule 'expr' ===")
    try:
        ast = model.parse(input_text, start="expr")
        print(f"SUCCESS: {ast}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"FAILED: {e}")

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS: Understanding the Problem<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Looking at the error and the debug output, here's what I understand:

1. The left recursion detection IS working - it's correctly identifying that both 'expr' and 'mul' are left-recursive and marking them as such.

2. The problem occurs in the parsing sequence:
   - When starting with 'mul', the parser tries to match 'expr' first
   - 'expr' can match 'mul', so there's a recursive call 
   - During the left recursion resolution process, something goes wrong

3. Looking at the stack trace:
   - The error happens in `_token('*')` - the parser expects to find '*' but doesn't
   - This suggests that during left recursion resolution, the parser is getting into a wrong state

4. The key difference between the two cases:
   - Starting with 'expr': expr -> mul -> expr '*' identifier (works)
   - Starting with 'mul': mul -> expr '*' identifier (fails)

The issue seems to be that when we start directly with a left-recursive rule ('mul'), the left recursion handling mechanism isn't properly dealing with the initial state.

Looking at the _recursive_call method, I suspect the issue is in how the left recursion guard and resolution works when the start rule itself is left-recursive.

Let me trace through what should happen:
1. Start parsing with 'mul'  
2. 'mul' rule is invoked: expr '*' identifier
3. 'expr' is parsed, which can be 'mul' (left recursion detected)
4. Left recursion handling should allow the proper resolution

The problem might be in the order of operations or the memoization logic when the start rule is itself left-recursive.</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Let me examine the left recursion handling more carefully. 1. **Working case (start with 'expr'):** The parser successfully resolves the left recursion and eventually matches `a * b`

2. **Failing case (start with 'mul'):** 
   - The parser enters `mul` → `expr` → `mul` (left recursion detected)
   - It resolves the recursion and tries `expr` → `identifier` → `'a'`
   - Then it successfully matches `'*'` and `'b'` 
   - But then it tries to match `mul` again at the top level and fails because it's looking for `'*'` at position 1:1 instead of continuing

The issue seems to be that after successfully parsing `a * b` within the recursive context, the parser tries to match another `mul` rule at the beginning, which fails because there's no `'*'` at the start.

Let me look more closely at the recursive call handling logic. The problem appears to be in how the results are memoized and retrieved.

Let me examine the `_recursive_call` method more carefully:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/neogeny__TatSu__4.2/tatsu/contexts.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>view</arg_value><arg_key>view_range</arg_key><arg_value>[540, 570]</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Here's the result of running `cat -n` on /workspace/neogeny__TatSu__4.2/tatsu/contexts.py:

   541	        pos = self._pos

   544	        if key in self._results:
   545	            return self._results[key]

</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Now I see the issue! In the `_recursive_call` method, when a rule is recursive, it goes through the iterative resolution process. However, there's a critical flaw:

1. Line 548: `if not self._is_recursive(ruleinfo.name):` - if the rule is NOT recursive, return early
2. Lines 551-562: Otherwise, it enters the while loop for recursive resolution
3. Lines 564-565: After resolution, it deletes the result from `_results` and forgets the key

But the problem is that when we start with a left-recursive rule directly, the deletion of the result on line 564 means that subsequent calls to the same rule won't find the previously computed result!

Let me trace through what happens:
1. We call `mul` (which is left-recursive)
2. It goes through the recursive resolution loop and finds a valid parse
3. The result is saved temporarily but then deleted on line 564
4. Later, when the outer parsing tries to validate the result, it calls `mul` again
5. But now `mul` has no memoized result, so it tries to parse again from scratch, which fails

The fix should be to NOT delete the final result from `_results` when we're done with the recursive resolution.

First, let me understand what kind of left recursion patterns work vs don't work:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/neogeny__TatSu__4.2/test_specific_case.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Test various left recursion patterns to understand the scope of the issue.
"""

from tatsu.tool import compile

def test_pattern(grammar, input_text, start_rules):
    """Test a grammar with different start rules."""
    print(f"Grammar:\n{grammar}")
    print(f"Input: {input_text}")
    
    model = compile(grammar, "TestGrammar")
    
    for start_rule in start_rules:
        print(f"\n--- Testing start rule '{start_rule}' ---")
        try:
            ast = model.parse(input_text, start=start_rule, trace=False)
            print(f"SUCCESS: {ast}")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"FAILED: {type(e).__name__}: {e}")

def main():
    # Pattern 1: The reported issue
    print("=" * 60)
    print("PATTERN 1: Original issue")
    print("=" * 60)
    grammar1 = '''
        identifier = /\w+/ ;
        expr = mul | identifier ;
        mul = expr '*' identifier ;
    '''
    test_pattern(grammar1, "a * b", ["expr", "mul"])
    
    # Pattern 2: Standard left recursion (should work)
    print("\n" + "=" * 60)
    print("PATTERN 2: Standard left recursion")
    print("=" * 60)
    grammar2 = '''
        @@left_recursion :: True
        start = expression $ ;
        expression
            =
            | expression '+' term
            | expression '*' term
            | term
            ;
        term = /\d+/ ;
    '''
    test_pattern(grammar2, "1 + 2 * 3", ["expression", "term"])
    
    # Pattern 3: Another indirect left recursion
    print("\n" + "=" * 60)
    print("PATTERN 3: Indirect left recursion similar to issue")  
    print("=" * 60)
    grammar3 = '''
        a = b | 'x' ;
        b = a 'y' ;
    '''
    test_pattern(grammar3, "x y", ["a", "b"])

if __name__ == "__main__":

[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Excellent! This confirms the pattern:

1. **Pattern 1 (original issue)**: `expr = mul | identifier; mul = expr '*' identifier` - fails when starting with `mul`
2. **Pattern 2 (standard)**: `expression = expression '+' term | ...` - works fine
3. **Pattern 3 (similar issue)**: `a = b | 'x'; b = a 'y'` - fails when starting with `b`

The common pattern in the failing cases is **indirect left recursion where the start rule itself participates in the left recursion cycle but is not the "primary" left-recursive rule**.

In Pattern 1: `mul` → `expr` → `mul` (cycle) but we start with `mul`
In Pattern 3: `b` → `a` → `b` (cycle) but we start with `b`

In Pattern 2: `expression` → `expression` (direct recursion) and we start with `expression` - this works.

So the issue is specifically when:
1. There's a left recursion cycle involving multiple rules
2. The start rule is one of the rules in the cycle but not necessarily the "entry point" that would normally be used

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS: Clear Problem Statement

### 5.1 What the problem is:
When parsing with a left-recursive rule that is not the typical "top-level" rule in a left-recursion cycle, the parser fails because it incorrectly removes the memoized result after resolving the left recursion.

### 5.2 Where the problem is located:
In `/workspace/neogeny__TatSu__4.2/tatsu/contexts.py`, in the `_recursive_call` method, lines 564-565:

```python
del self._results[key]
self._forget(key)
```

These lines remove the final result from memoization, causing subsequent calls to the same rule to fail.

### 5.3 How the test reproduces the issue:
The reproduction script creates a grammar where `mul = expr '*' identifier` and `expr = mul | identifier`, forming an indirect left recursion. When parsing `"a * b"` starting with rule `mul`, the parser:
1. Detects left recursion between `mul` and `expr`
2. Resolves it correctly internally
3. But deletes the memoized result
4. Later tries to validate the parse by calling `mul` again, which fails

### 5.4 Best practices for the fix:
- Preserve existing functionality for all working cases
- Make minimal changes to the core parsing logic
- Ensure the fix doesn't break memoization for non-recursive cases
- The fix should be consistent with how other left-recursive cases work

### 5.5 How to fix the problem:
The fix is to NOT delete the final result from `_results` after left recursion resolution. The result should remain memoized so that subsequent calls to the same rule at the same position can reuse it.

However, we need to be careful - we can't just remove the deletion entirely, as that might cause issues with the memoization cache. Let me examine this more carefully.

Let me add some debug to see what's in the results cache:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/neogeny__TatSu__4.2/debug_results.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Debug script to understand the results cache behavior.
"""

import tatsu
from tatsu.tool import compile

# Monkey patch to add debug prints
original_recursive_call = tatsu.contexts.ParseContext._recursive_call

def debug_recursive_call(self, ruleinfo):
    pos = self._pos
    key = self.memokey
    
    print(f"_recursive_call: {ruleinfo.name} at pos {pos}, key={key}")
    if key in self._results:
        print(f"  Found in _results: {self._results[key]}")
        return self._results[key]
    
    print(f"  Not in _results, invoking rule")
    result = self._invoke_rule(ruleinfo, pos, key)
    
    if not self._is_recursive(ruleinfo.name):
        print(f"  Not recursive, returning result")
        return result
    
    print(f"  Is recursive, entering resolution loop")
    iteration = 0
    while True:
        iteration += 1
        print(f"    Iteration {iteration}")
        self._forget(key)
        self._save_result(key, result.node)
        print(f"    Saved result, _results now: {list(self._results.keys())}")
        self._clear_recursion_errors()
        self._goto(pos)
        try:
            new_result = self._invoke_rule(ruleinfo, pos, key)
            print(f"    New result obtained")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"    Invoke failed: {e}")
            break
        if self._pos <= result.newpos:
            print(f"    Position not advanced, breaking")
            break
        print(f"    Position advanced, continuing")
        result = new_result

    print(f"  Resolution complete, deleting result for {key}")
    if key in self._results:
        print(f"    Result was: {self._results[key]}")
    del self._results[key]
    self._forget(key)
    print(f"  Returning final result")
    return result

# Apply monkey patch
tatsu.contexts.ParseContext._recursive_call = debug_recursive_call

def test_debug():
    grammar = '''
        identifier = /\w+/ ;
        expr = mul | identifier ;
        mul = expr '*' identifier ;
    '''
    
    input_text = "a * b"
    
    model = compile(grammar, "TestGrammar")
    
    print("=== Testing start rule 'expr' (should work) ===")
    try:
        ast = model.parse(input_text, start="expr")
        print(f"SUCCESS: {ast}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"FAILED: {e}")
        
    print("\n=== Testing start rule 'mul' (currently fails) ===") 
    try:
        ast = model.parse(input_text, start="mul")
        print(f"SUCCESS: {ast}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"FAILED: {e}")

[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Perfect! Now I can clearly see the problem. Let me analyze the key differences:

**Working case (start with 'expr'):**
1. `expr` calls `mul` 
2. `mul` calls `expr` (left recursion detected)
3. `expr` resolves its recursion, saves result, **deletes it**
4. `mul` resolves its recursion, saves result, **deletes it**  
5. But wait... there's more! The outer `expr` call succeeds and returns the final result

**Failing case (start with 'mul'):**
1. `mul` calls `expr`
2. `expr` calls `mul` (left recursion detected)  
3. `mul` resolves its recursion, saves result, **deletes it**
4. `expr` resolves its recursion, saves result, **deletes it**
5. But then the original `mul` call needs to validate its result, so it calls `mul` again
6. This time `mul` is NOT found in `_results` because it was deleted!
7. So `mul` tries to parse from scratch and fails

The issue is that when we start directly with a left-recursive rule, the deletion of the final result prevents subsequent validation.

However, looking more carefully at the successful case, I notice that there are TWO recursive resolutions happening:
1. First `expr` resolves its recursion 
2. Then `mul` resolves its recursion

Both delete their results, but somehow it still works. Let me trace this more carefully...

Actually, let me check if the issue is that we shouldn't delete the result at all. Let me look at what other PEG parsers do. But first, let me understand WHY the deletion was put there.

Looking at the algorithm logic:
1. During recursive resolution, temporary results are saved
2. The resolution loop continues until no more progress is made
3. At the end, the final result should be available for future use

The deletion seems wrong. The result should remain memoized. Let me check if there are any comments or documentation about this.

But first, let me try a simple fix - just commenting out the deletion lines:

"""
Debug script with current fix to see what's happening.
"""

import tatsu
from tatsu.tool import compile

# Monkey patch to add debug prints
original_recursive_call = tatsu.contexts.ParseContext._recursive_call

def debug_recursive_call(self, ruleinfo):
    pos = self._pos
    key = self.memokey
    
    print(f"_recursive_call: {ruleinfo.name} at pos {pos}, key={key}")
    if key in self._results:
        print(f"  Found in _results: {self._results[key]}")
        return self._results[key]
    
    print(f"  Not in _results, invoking rule")
    result = self._invoke_rule(ruleinfo, pos, key)
    
    if not self._is_recursive(ruleinfo.name):
        print(f"  Not recursive, returning result")
        return result
    
    print(f"  Is recursive, entering resolution loop")
    iteration = 0
    while True:
        iteration += 1
        print(f"    Iteration {iteration}")
        self._forget(key)
        self._save_result(key, result.node)
        print(f"    Saved result, _results now: {list(self._results.keys())}")
        self._clear_recursion_errors()
        self._goto(pos)
        try:
            new_result = self._invoke_rule(ruleinfo, pos, key)
            print(f"    New result obtained")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"    Invoke failed: {e}")
            break
        if self._pos <= result.newpos:
            print(f"    Position not advanced, breaking")
            break
        print(f"    Position advanced, continuing")
        result = new_result

    print(f"  Resolution complete")
    if key in self._results:
        print(f"    Final result will be: {self._results[key]}")
    else:
        print(f"    No result found in _results")
    # With our fix, we don't delete the result
    # del self._results[key]
    # self._forget(key)
    print(f"  Returning final result (NOT deleting due to fix)")
    return result

# Apply monkey patch
tatsu.contexts.ParseContext._recursive_call = debug_recursive_call

def test_debug():
    grammar = '''
        identifier = /\w+/ ;
        expr = mul | identifier ;
        mul = expr '*' identifier ;
    '''
    
    input_text = "a * b"
    
    model = compile(grammar, "TestGrammar")
    
    print("=== Testing start rule 'mul' (should now work) ===") 
    try:
        ast = model.parse(input_text, start="mul")
        print(f"SUCCESS: {ast}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"FAILED: {e}")
        import traceback
        traceback.print_exc()

Looking at the failing case again:
1. We start parsing `mul` on input `"a * b"`
2. `mul` calls `expr`, which can call `mul` - left recursion detected
3. Left recursion resolution computes the correct result `['a', '*', 'b']` and stores it
4. The result gets deleted from `_results` 
5. Later, the main parsing tries to validate by calling `mul` again
6. But now there's no memoized result, so it tries to parse fresh and fails

The key insight is that the deletion happens in the context of resolving the left recursion, but the main parsing context still needs that result.

But why don't other left recursion tests have this problem? Let me look at the working test case more carefully.

2. `expr` calls `mul`
3. `mul` calls `expr` - left recursion
4. Left recursion resolution happens for both `expr` and `mul`

Let me look at this from the perspective of what the left recursion algorithm should be doing:

For `mul = expr '*' identifier` where `expr = mul | identifier`:
- `mul` is NOT directly left-recursive (it doesn't start with `mul`)
- But `mul` is INDIRECTLY left-recursive because `expr` can be `mul`

The left recursion detection should identify that there's a cycle: `mul` → `expr` → `mul`.

So when parsing `mul`:
1. Try to parse `expr` 
2. `expr` can be `mul`, so we detect the cycle
3. We need to resolve this cycle

The resolution algorithm should work like this:
1. Initially assume `mul` parses nothing (empty result)
2. Try parsing with this assumption: `expr` (which tries `mul` and gets empty result) then `'*'` then `identifier`
3. This fails because we can't match `'*'` after the empty `expr` result
4. So we try again with a better approximation...
